GUIDE:
# 7B. Ruler Personal Integrity and Honesty

Status: draft; v5 objective-evidence questions require controlled rejudgment; v1 invalid

## Chapter Identity

- Chapter: `7B`
- Category: `ruler_personal_integrity_and_honesty`
- Evidence strategy: one ruler-period dossier, researched across ten overlapping lenses
- Judge strategy: one chapter judge applies one comparative meter across all eligible rulers
- Rubric version: `chapter_7b_v5`

## Chapter Purpose

Assess whether the ruler uses public office honestly and for public rather than
personal, family, business, factional, or patronage advantage. The chapter joins
truthfulness, correction, conflicts of interest, self-enrichment, appointments,
independent scrutiny, concealment, commitments, favoritism, and ethical example
into one final 1-10 integrity score.

The ten questions are evidence lenses, not ten equally weighted sub-scores.
Evidence may illuminate several lenses, and missing evidence for one lens lowers
confidence rather than invalidating the dossier or preventing a chapter score.

## Ten Evidence Lenses

| Lens | Simple question | Detailed research question | Priority evidence |
|---|---|---|---|
| **7B.1 — Truthfulness** | Did the ruler tell the truth when lying could protect their power or reputation? | Does the ruler tell the truth in verifiable public statements, formal records, legislative testimony, courts, and international commitments, especially when deception would protect power, benefit, or reputation? | **Rhetoric and representations**; **Formal acts and law** |
| **7B.2 — Correcting falsehoods and errors** | Did the ruler admit and correct false claims and mistakes? | When reliable records, courts, audits, or investigations expose error or falsehood, does the ruler correct the record, comply, and remedy harm rather than retaliate, conceal, or knowingly repeat the claim? | **Rhetoric and representations**; **Implementation and operational conduct**; **Outcomes** |
| **7B.3 — Conflicts of interest** | Did the ruler keep personal and family interests separate from public decisions? | Does the ruler support and personally comply with conflict-of-interest, disclosure, recusal, divestment, and ethics rules separating personal, family, and business interests from state decisions? | **Formal acts and law**; **Implementation and operational conduct**; **Outcomes** |
| **7B.4 — Personal profit from office** | Did the ruler or close family improperly profit from public office? | Do asset, tax, gift, ownership, contract, foundation, emolument, bribe, insider-access, and legal records show that the ruler or close family profited from office, and did the ruler permit final findings, recovery, and accountability? | **Resources**; **Implementation and operational conduct**; **Outcomes** |
| **7B.5 — Nepotism and loyalist appointments** | Did the ruler choose officials for competence rather than personal loyalty or connections? | Do the ruler's appointments and removals reflect competence and lawful process, or family, friendship, donations, business ties, and loyalty used to protect personal power or self-dealing? | **Personnel**; **Implementation and operational conduct**; **Outcomes** |
| **7B.6 — Independent investigation** | Could independent institutions investigate the ruler and close associates? | Did the ruler preserve the law, jurisdiction, appointments, funding, and access needed for independent investigation of their conduct, assets, campaigns, associates, and concealed decisions? | **Formal acts and law**; **Personnel**; **Implementation and operational conduct** |
| **7B.7 — Obstruction and concealment** | Did the ruler obey accountability processes rather than obstruct or conceal wrongdoing? | Did the ruler comply with subpoenas, judgments, audits, and disclosure duties, or use vetoes, decrees, pardons, dismissals, secrecy, or retaliation to conceal conduct and obstruct accountability? | **Formal acts and law**; **Implementation and operational conduct**; **Rhetoric and representations** |
| **7B.8 — Promises and good faith** | Did the ruler keep commitments and explain changes honestly? | Do the ruler's documented legislative positions, formal commitments, and implemented decisions show consistent good-faith promises, or opportunistic reversal and concealed tradeoffs for personal advantage? | **Rhetoric and representations**; **Formal acts and law**; **Implementation and operational conduct** |
| **7B.9 — Favoritism and clientelism** | Did the ruler avoid using public power to reward favored people and networks? | Did the ruler personally direct, benefit from, knowingly tolerate, or correct favoritism and clientelism in procurement, licensing, pardons, enforcement, and privileged access? | **Resources**; **Implementation and operational conduct**; **Outcomes** |
| **7B.10 — Ethical example and public trust** | Did the ruler's conduct strengthen ethical standards and public trust? | Did the ruler's personal conduct and support for durable integrity institutions strengthen public trust, or normalize lying, impunity, self-dealing, conflicts, and cynicism? | **Rhetoric and representations**; **Implementation and operational conduct**; **Outcomes** |

Priority evidence categories indicate promising evidence, not required coverage or an
exclusive source list. Missing categories do not invalidate a lens.

## Scope and Non-Goals

- Judge conduct attributable to the ruler, including conduct knowingly directed,
  enabled, protected, or tolerated by the ruler's close governing network.
- Do not infer personal dishonesty from a country's inherited corruption level.
- Do not equate policy disagreement, honest error, changed policy after new
  evidence, rhetoric, secrecy justified by genuine security/privacy/diplomatic
  needs, or an unproved allegation with deception or corruption.
- Do not reward polished communications, formal ethics rules, prosecutions of
  rivals, or selective transparency without evidence of genuine practice.
- Do not score general competence, prosperity, freedom, or popularity except
  where the facts directly demonstrate integrity or abuse of office.
- Never use the client matrix as evidence.

Country corruption indicators, authoritarian institutions, censorship, detention,
human-rights abuse, and political-freedom conditions are context only unless the
evidence establishes a direct nexus to ruler-specific deception, personal or network
benefit, favoritism, concealment, obstruction of scrutiny, retaliation against an
integrity investigation, or deliberate abuse of office. Lenses 7B.7 and 7B.10 do not
convert every repressive act into personal dishonesty. Score those acts primarily in
3B/4B unless that integrity nexus is evidenced.

Apply this as an item-level admissibility gate, not merely a caveat after scoring. For
each potentially decisive item, identify the ruler's personal statement, interest,
benefit, appointment, protected network, response to scrutiny, concealment, correction,
obstruction, or knowing tolerance after notice. If none is shown, the item receives zero
scoring weight even when it proves authoritarianism, repression, country corruption, or
weak institutions. Do not cite a zero-weight item as decisive or use it to reject an
adjacent anchor.

## Researcher Workflow and Evidence Themes

Follow the local-first researcher guide and source-confidence registry. Query the
exact ruler/country/year or bounded period locally before web discovery. Treat
WGI, V-Dem, and CPI as country-level context and triangulation, not proof of the
ruler's personal conduct.

Research by themes that cut across lenses, not by ten isolated searches:

1. Material public statements, corrections, sworn evidence, and documented
   concealment, including examples of costly candor and adverse findings.
2. Assets, beneficial ownership, gifts, business/family interests, procurement,
   licensing, regulation, pardons, campaign finance, and foreign-policy benefits.
3. Appointments and access patterns: competence, kinship, donor/client ties,
   loyalty demands, and whether appointees facilitate self-dealing or impunity.
4. Courts, prosecutors, auditors, legislatures, ethics bodies, inquiries,
   journalists, and whistleblowers: independence, obstruction, retaliation,
   remedies, and ruler response.
5. Promise and commitment records, ethical rules, institutional reforms, and
   whether practice strengthened or normalized trust and accountability.

Prefer primary legal and financial records, judgments, audit/inquiry findings,
legislative records, asset declarations, procurement records, official
transcripts, sanctions findings with stated evidence, reputable investigative
reporting, and serious academic histories. Official sources are strong for the
fact that a statement, appointment, filing, or rule exists, but conditional for
self-serving claims of honesty. Allegations must remain allegations; seek an
independent finding, underlying record, response, and contrary evidence.

Each accepted item should retain its citation, date, claim, period fit, source
type/confidence, ruler-attribution assessment, and uncertainty. Reuse a stable
evidence item across every relevant lens. Coverage descriptions may use natural
language; no exact status vocabulary is required.

## Common Evidence-Item Semantics

Each material evidence item should carry a stable evidence ID; a concise factual
claim; source URL, title, publisher, and publication/access date where available;
source type and confidence with a short reason; a quotation or precise locator
when available; target-year/ruler-period fit; ruler attribution and
inherited-baseline relevance; its positive, negative, mixed, contextual, or
contrary role; and the supported lens IDs. Fields may be normalized later and
natural-language equivalents are acceptable. The substantive minimum is a
traceable source, intelligible claim, temporal fit, attribution, and evidence
role. One item may support multiple lenses without duplication.

## Attribution, Sparse Evidence, and Historical Cases

Separate personal direction or tolerance from inherited institutions and acts of
family, ministers, parties, businesses, or security services. Consider formal and
informal authority, time in office, credible knowledge, ability to intervene,
action after notice, coalition constraints, and whether the ruler benefited.
Close-associate misconduct is not automatically the ruler's, but repeated benefit,
protection, appointment, obstruction, or failure to act after credible notice can
support attribution.

For older, obscure, closed, short-tenure, or poorly archived cases, use the best
available contemporary records and reputable later scholarship. Do not interpret
silence as integrity where reporting and accountability were suppressed. A dossier
with only a few strong themes can still be judged; narrow coverage, one-sided
archives, weak attribution, or missing adverse-case searches should reduce
confidence and may widen the defensible score range. Do not manufacture lens
coverage or impose a modern disclosure regime anachronistically.

Silence, favorable institutions, or the absence of reported allegations cannot prove
integrity. Scores above 5 require affirmative ruler-specific evidence of candor,
separation of interests, correction, professional appointments, or acceptance of
genuine scrutiny. Scores below 4 require ruler-specific recurring deception,
self-dealing, favoritism, patronage, concealment, or obstruction. If neither side is
supported by traceable ruler-specific conduct, return null rather than rewarding or
punishing the information environment.

### Absolute-scale and visibility guardrails

The annual cohort does not define the endpoints. Use fixed cross-period reference
patterns rather than assigning the most opaque current ruler to the floor:

- **1:** Personal or family extraction, deliberate deception, cronyism, and
  state-backed concealment or impunity are central instruments of rule, supported by
  direct findings or strong convergent ruler-specific evidence. Mobutu-era kleptocracy
  or Marcos-family extraction are reference patterns; generic authoritarian control is
  insufficient.
- **2–3:** Severe or recurring ruler-specific deception, conflicts, favoritism,
  self-dealing, or obstruction spans important domains, without reaching that floor.
- **4–5:** Genuinely mixed personal conduct: attributable safeguards or honest conduct
  coexist with material lapses, patronage, conflicts, broken commitments, or weak
  accountability.
- **6–7:** Generally honest public service with real scrutiny and correction, but
  notable attributable lapses or opacity remain.
- **8–9:** Sustained candor, separation of interests, professional appointments, and
  acceptance of independent scrutiny, including evidence under adverse incentives.
- **10:** Exceptional costly candor and durable strengthening against self-dealing and
  impunity under strong contrary incentives.

Normalize visibility before scoring. Numerous fact checks, press controversies, or
allegations in an open system do not establish a worse pattern than silence in a closed
system. Conversely, closed-system silence does not establish integrity or personal
corruption. Compare materiality, repetition, findings, personal benefit, response, and
remedy—not article counts. When admissible personal-nexus evidence is thin on both
sides, return null rather than converting repression into a low score or silence into a
midpoint.

## Holistic 1-10 Chapter Rubric

Synthesize the ruler's pattern, materiality, intent, personal benefit, use of state
power, duration, corrective behavior, and institutional effect. Severe direct
self-dealing or obstruction may properly dominate several weaker positive lenses;
conversely, an isolated error should not outweigh a broad record of candor and
accountability. Do not average lens labels or count citations.

| Score | Chapter anchor |
|---:|---|
| 1 | Deliberate deception, personal or family extraction, cronyism, and state-backed concealment/impunity are central instruments of rule and materially corrupt public office. |
| 2-3 | Severe or recurring dishonesty, conflicts, self-dealing, favoritism, or obstruction span important domains; independent accountability is attacked or largely ineffective. |
| 4-5 | A material mixed record: meaningful integrity safeguards or honest conduct coexist with recurring deception, patronage, conflicts, broken commitments, or weak accountability. |
| 6-7 | Generally honest and public-serving conduct with real scrutiny and correction, but notable lapses, opacity, favoritism, conflicts, or accountability weaknesses remain. |
| 8-9 | Strong, sustained truthfulness, separation of interests, professional public service, openness to scrutiny, correction, and ethical example; credible lapses are rare and remedied. |
| 10 | Exceptional and durable integrity under adverse incentives: costly candor, exemplary separation of interests, robust independent accountability, and institutions strengthened against self-dealing and impunity. |

## Confidence

Report confidence separately on a 0-100 scale. Base it on source authority and
independence, ruler attribution, temporal fit, information environment, diversity
of themes and source types, contrary-evidence search, and agreement. Evidence
volume alone is not confidence. Missing lenses normally lower confidence; they do
not mechanically lower the chapter score. State the most plausible score range
when uncertainty is material.

## One-Judge Comparative Workflow

One judge owns 7B for the target year/period across all eligible rulers:

1. Read every dossier's 7B evidence and explicit gaps without demanding uniform
   coverage terminology or perfect mapping tables.
2. Separate identity-quarantined cases; retain sparse cases with reduced
   confidence rather than discarding them.
3. Establish provisional low, middle, and high batch anchors using diverse
   regimes, periods, visibility levels, and evidence environments.
4. Make tentative holistic scores, then compare neighboring cases for consistent
   thresholds of intent, materiality, personal benefit, obstruction, correction,
   and attribution.
5. Revisit outliers and cases whose score rests mainly on country indicators,
   official self-report, accusation volume, or media visibility.
6. Emit one final chapter score, confidence/range, rationale, strongest positive
   and negative evidence, missing themes, contrary evidence, and review flag.

## Common Chapter-Judge Output Envelope

The judge returns one object per ruler using these exact semantic field names so
all eight chapter outputs can be normalized by the same inexpensive formatter:

- `chapter_id`: `7B`
- `rubric_version`: `chapter_7b_v5`
- `calibration_batch_id` and `calibrated_against`
- `score_1_to_10`, or null with `insufficient_evidence_reason`
- `confidence_score` and `plausible_score_range`
- `decisive_positive_evidence` and `decisive_negative_evidence`, referencing
  stable evidence IDs
- `inherited_baseline_and_constraints` and `ruler_attribution`
- `supported_lenses` and `missing_or_weak_lenses`
- `contrary_evidence`
- `source_mix` and `structured_prior_summary`
- `chapter_rationale`, `lower_anchor_rejected`, and `higher_anchor_rejected`
- `manual_review_required`, `manual_review_reason_type`, and `manual_review_reason`

Harmless serialization differences can be repaired after judgment, but the
semantic envelope must be complete. A null score is reserved for evidence too
thin or untraceable to support any responsible range, unresolved identity, or
attribution too uncertain to judge—not merely missing lenses or flexible coverage
wording.

## Chapter-Specific Calibration

Use the common bias checks in `cited-evaluation-calibration.md`, interpreted at
chapter level, plus:

- `rubric_version`: `chapter_7b_v5`
- `integrity_pattern`: `systemic_abuse`, `recurring_serious_abuse`, `mixed`,
  `generally_high_integrity`, `exceptional_integrity`, or `unclear`
- `deception_pattern`: `strategic_systematic`, `recurring_material`, `mixed`,
  `limited`, `not_shown`, or `unclear`
- `personal_benefit_evidence`: `direct`, `strong_inference`, `limited`,
  `not_shown`, or `unclear`
- `accountability_response`: `obstructed_or_retalitated`, `impunity`, `partial`,
  `accepted_and_corrected`, `strengthened`, or `unclear`
- `conflict_and_patronage_pattern`: `systemic`, `recurring`, `mixed`, `limited`,
  `not_shown`, or `unclear`
- `ruler_attribution`: concise assessment of direction, knowledge, benefit,
  protection/tolerance, authority, and action after notice
- `lens_strengths` and `lens_gaps`: concise ID lists with natural-language notes
- `defensible_score_range`: lower and upper score under current evidence

Explicitly check visibility/fact-checking bias, silence under repression,
allegation-versus-finding, family/associate attribution, repetition of one scandal,
anachronistic disclosure expectations, and whether national corruption conditions
were wrongly personalized.

## Smoke-Test Set

These are calibration roles, not predetermined scores.

| Ruler | Country | Period | Calibration purpose |
|---|---|---|---|
| Richard Nixon | United States | 1972-1974 | documented deception, concealment, institutions, and remedy |
| Donald Trump | United States | 2017-2020 | high visibility, business conflicts, appointments, and fact-check volume |
| Nelson Mandela | South Africa | 1994-1999 | high-integrity candidate requiring genuine adverse search |
| Lee Kuan Yew | Singapore | 1965-1990 | low-corruption governance with constrained scrutiny |
| Ferdinand Marcos | Philippines | 1972-1986 | family enrichment, patronage, authoritarian archive |
| Angela Merkel | Germany | 2005-2021 | long-tenure, high-anchor candidate and policy-change boundary |
| Vladimir Putin | Russia | 2000-2022 | closed environment, associates, concealment, and attribution |
| José Mujica | Uruguay | 2010-2015 | modest-lifestyle reputation versus institutional evidence |
| Mobutu Sese Seko | Zaire | 1965-1997 | personal rule and extraction in a sparse historical record |
| Jacinda Ardern | New Zealand | 2020-2022 | crisis candor, correction, and high-visibility scrutiny |
| Ellen Johnson Sirleaf | Liberia | 2006-2018 | post-conflict institutions, family allegations, and lower-visibility evidence |
| Indira Gandhi | India | 1966-1977 | historical woman ruler, emergency accountability, and non-Western archives |
| Maria Liberia-Peters | Netherlands Antilles | 1984-1986 | lower-visibility woman ruler and sparse-evidence confidence discipline |

## Acceptance Checklist

- Every numeric score requires direct ruler-specific integrity evidence: a personal
  act, disclosure, conflict response, truthfulness episode, accountability choice, or
  comparably attributable pattern. Generic repression, institutional strength, and
  absence of reported scandal are context only. Without the personal nexus, return
  null rather than `5`; evidence explicitly described as absent cannot support above
  `5`.

- All ten authoritative lenses are present verbatim and treated as overlapping,
  non-equal evidence lenses.
- Local-first order, source-confidence fields, citations, and run profile are
  retained.
- Evidence items satisfy the common traceability, temporal-fit, attribution,
  evidence-role, and lens-link semantics.
- Ruler conduct is separated from inherited national corruption and associate
  conduct is attributed explicitly.
- Allegations, findings, honest error, secrecy, deception, correction, benefit,
  and obstruction are distinguished.
- Missing lenses reduce confidence rather than invalidate or mechanically depress
  the score.
- One judge compares all rulers with one holistic rubric and records adjacent-case
  calibration.
- Final output uses the common chapter-judge envelope, including adjacent-anchor
  comparisons and an explicit manual-review flag.
- Smoke testing and revision occur before production activation.

"source_id": "SRC-c0d225ca90a4346a", "url": "https://documents1.worldbank.org/curated/en/556411654274244589/pdf/Mexico-Inclusive-and-Sustainable-Economic-Growth-Development-Policy-Loan.pdf", "title": "Mexico Inclusive and Sustainable Economic Growth Development Policy Loan", "publisher": "World Bank", "raw_sha256": "ec19b96f3781e708713ba3527f80235331cdc3ca896f3e637d27a75e528751d2", "fact_summary": "A November 2021 presidential accord declared certain infrastructure projects matters of national security and public interest and ordered provisional environmental and social permits within five business days. The Supreme Court partially suspended the accord after an INAI challenge; SEMARNAT said it had not yet been implemented and continued applying the environmental framework, but the World Bank judged potential implementation a significant risk to environmental safeguards and reform outcomes.", "question_ids": ["3B.4", "3B.9", "4B.4", "4B.9", "5B.7", "7B.6", "7B.7", "8B.8", "8B.9"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "The accord and litigation predate 2023 but were identified as an ongoing implementation risk for the period.", "ruler_attribution": "The accord was issued by López Obrador as president.", "limitations": ["The source states the accord had not been implemented as of the document's preparation; it therefore shows exposure and institutional conflict more than realized harm."], "start_unit": 45, "end_unit": 46, "locator": "page 45 through page 46", "exact_excerpt": "The World Bank\nMexico Inclusive and Sustainable Economic Growth DPL (P178224)\nareas that were previously deforested. As a result, vulnerable populations living in previously deforested\nlands could be negatively affected by the new guidelines in the short run. The long-term impact of a new\nnatural protected area in the San Miguelito mountains is expected to have positive poverty and social\nimpacts by reducing climate change impacts, including reductions in the risk of forest fires and flooding\nthat could be devastating to populated areas. Given that the proposed area includes multiple zones that\nwould allow for some productive activities, the short-run poverty and social impacts are expected to be\nneutral.\n5.2. ENVIRONMENTAL, FORESTS, AND OTHER NATURAL RESOURCE ASPECTS\n76. Policy measures supported by the DPL are expected to have significant positive effects on\nclimate change and the environment. The WBG’s environmental analysis of the DPL finds that most\npolicies supported, mainly under pillar 2, would have significant positive effects on Mexico’s climate\nchange mitigation and environmental priorities. Policy measures of pillar 2 will enhance the\nenvironmental and climate change resilience under existing laws and programs that promote\nenvironmental sustainability and disaster risk management. Mexico’s institutional and regulatory\nframework to avoid, reduce, mitigate, and manage adverse environmental effects is sufficiently robust\nand can address potential negative environmental effects arising from these policy reforms.\n77. The policy measures will support Mexico in complying with its NDC by fostering low carbon and\nclimate-resilient growth, enhancing NPA management and forest conservation, and establishing\neffective disaster risk management. These policy measures will specifically strengthen the country’s\nadaptation capacity, particularly in the most vulnerable municipalities, and enhance resilience of urban\nand rural landscapes. The DPL will foster mandatory mitigation measures for land use changes in forest\nlands (PA 5), enforce measures to avoid deforestation (PA 6), and enhance Mexico’s area under\nconservation status (PA 8), contributing to the preservation of carbon sinks that are essential for Mexico\nto meet its NDC. The DPL will also strengthen disaster risk management (PA 4) which is expected to have\npositive environmental effects by fostering enhanced resilience and disaster responsiveness nationally.\nPolicy reforms under pillar 1 around digital financial inclusion could have longer-term positive\nenvironmental effects.\n78. Mexico has a solid and comprehensive national environmental legislation to promote\nsustainability and resilience measures supported by this operation. The legislation was formally\ndeveloped based on the General Law of Ecological Equilibrium and Environmental Protection (LGEEPA) of\n1988. Since then, Mexico has established laws, policies, programs, and provided resources that set\nenvironmental requirements to investments in terms of managing environmental impacts and waste,\ncontrolling pollution, and addressing material efficiency and climate change, among other. Regarding the\nlatter, the General Climate Change Law of 2012 supports the transition to a competitive, sustainable, and\nlow-carbon economy; reduces the vulnerability of the population and ecosystems; and assigns\nresponsibilities within the Government. The GoM has committed to supporting climate change mitigation\nand adaptation as reflected in its updated NDC under the United Nations Framework Convention on\nClimate Change.\n79. On November 22, 2021, the GoM issued a Presidential Accord declaring the execution of certain\ninfrastructure projects a matter of national security and public interest and ordered federal agencies to\nPage 41\n\nThe World Bank\nMexico Inclusive and Sustainable Economic Growth DPL (P178224)\nissue provisional environmental and social permits within five business days from the time of the\nrequest, to expedite the execution of said projects. On December 14, 2021, the Mexican Supreme Court\npartially suspended the Presidential Accord in response to the constitutional controversy raised by the\nNational Institute of Transparency, Access to Information, and Protection of Personal Data (INAI) with\nrespect to any effects and consequences of the Presidential Accord that may derive from the classification\nof information as of public interest and/or national security. These legal challenges may take up to a year\nto be resolved, and project-specific provisional permits issued could be challenged by affected parties in\ncourts, seeking temporary suspension of the provisions of the Accord, or an injunction on project-specific\nrelated activities, until courts make a final decision. SEMARNAT has informed that, so far, the Accord has\nnot been implemented since approval and that it continues to apply Mexico’s environmental management\nframework, confirming that all environmental legislation remains valid and in place, and would be\nenforced during the operation’s timeline. However, the potential implementation of the Accord poses a\nsignificant risk that could weaken Mexico’s environmental management framework and undermine the\npolicy measures supported by the DPL under Pillar 2 (see paragraph 95).\n5.3. PFM, DISBURSEMENT AND AUDITING ASPECTS\n80. Mexico’s Public Financial Management (PFM) framework is mostly aligned with good\ninternational practices, including the use of budget resources and the FOREX internal control system\nmanaged by the Mexican Central Bank. According to the latest Fiscal Transparency Evaluation (FTE) of\nthe IMF (dated October 2018), Mexico scores relatively well compared to other Latin American countries\nand emerging market economies that have undergone such evaluations. Out of the 48 principles across\nfour pillars in the FTE, Mexico meets 16 principles at the basic level, 9 principles at the good level, and 15\nprinciples at the advanced level. The areas of strength include fiscal reporting, forecasting, and budgeting\nand resource revenue management. Conversely, the areas for improvement include the need to\nstrengthen the medium-term fiscal framework, the medium-term budget framework, and fiscal risk\nanalysis and management.\n81. Government commitment to support PFM reforms. Mexico’s budget legal and control\nframework is comprehensive, and the country has demonstrated a good track record in implementing\nPFM reforms. One of the recent major reforms focused on public sector accountability, integrity, and\ntransparency with the establishment of the Law63 of the National Anti-Corruption System, which aims to\nimprove coordination among different oversight entities and civil society organizations.64 However,\ncorruption control is an ongoing concern in the public sector.65 In addition, Mexico adopted accrual\naccounting, harmonizing accounting standards across all levels of government through the creation of the\nNational Council of Accounting Harmonization (Consejo Nacional de Armonización Contable) and the\nenactment of the General Government Accounting Law in 2008.\n63 The Law of the National Anti-Corruption System was issued in 2016 and reformed in 2021.\n64 The members of the National Anti-Corruption System include representatives from civil society, the heads of the Supreme\nAudit Institution, the Ministry of Public Administration, the anti-corruption agency, and INAI, among others.\n65 Mexico scored 31 out of 100 in the Corruption Perception Index prepared by Transparency International, below most of its\nregional peers.\nPage 42", "excerpt_sha256": Localizar a una persona desaparecida, pero finada, incrementa las cifras de homicidio, lo mismo ocurre si se localiza una\nfosa clandestina, por lo que la autoridad tiene pocos incentivos para destinar recursos a la localización de personas, pues “sin cuerpo no hay delito”.", "excerpt_sha256": "1c5e4e6025b4e98ea99f38149387817eb1f648c0e9fb33516e27822dc044dcad", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0016-E010", "source_id": "SRC-accb4d26e6454348", "url": "https://www.mexicoevalua.org/wp-content/uploads/2024/12/balance-seguridad-amlo.pdf", "title": "Balance de seguridad de AMLO", "publisher": "México Evalúa", "raw_sha256": "2f1a695902cb4740bd101b6f5c7db4f28671eae35f56100e65c9f8e608cb103e", "fact_summary": "From 2018 to 2023, personnel assigned to Mexico’s prosecutors’ offices fell 8%, FGR spending fell 28.4% through 2024, and prosecutorial administrative units fell 22.6%; pending investigations rose 60.3%. Illegal detentions remained above 20% at the state level on average and reached 11% federally on average across the reported period. The IMF recommended stronger coordination, resources, sanctions, asset recovery, and verification of beneficial ownership in procurement.", "question_ids": ["5B.5", "5B.7", "7B.10", "8B.6"], "polarity": "adverse", "period_fit": "The findings concern the institutional and enforcement status reviewed in 2023.", "ruler_attribution": "The report evaluates Mexico's anti-corruption institutions and authorities during AMLO's administration; it does not establish personal enrichment by López Obrador.", "limitations": ["The evidence does not prove that AMLO personally directed corruption or benefited from it."], "start_unit": 88, "end_unit": 92, "locator": "page 88 through page 92", "exact_excerpt": "MEXICO\n\n\n\n     Annex VIII. Leveraging the Anti-Money Laundering Efforts to\n                   Support Anti-Corruption Efforts1\n\nA. Interaction between Mexico’s AML and AC Frameworks\n\n1.      Corruption and money laundering (ML) are symbiotic as they tend to co-occur. The\npresence of one tends to create and reciprocally reinforce the incidence of the other. 2 The strongest\nlink between the two crimes is the need for corruption proceeds to be laundered by the criminals,\nwhich implicates the AML framework. Some of the AML measures—such as the enhanced due\ndiligence measures applicable to Politically Exposed Persons (PEPs) that pose high risk for corruption\nand the filing of Suspicious Transaction Reports (STRs) by reportable entities—could, in turn, be\nhelpful in detecting and preventing corruption in the first place. Furthermore, corruption could\nundermine the proper functioning and effectiveness of the AML/CFT framework (e.g., independence\nand governance of AML/CFT institutions). It is therefore important that frameworks aimed at\ntackling corruption and money laundering work in tandem to tackle financial crimes and limit their\nimpact on the macroeconomy to help ensure a sustainable and inclusive economic growth and\npromote a conducive investment environment.\n\n2.      The 2020 National Risk Assessment on ML and terrorism financing ranked laundering\nof corruption proceeds among the highest risks in Mexico. The preliminary results from the most\nrecent update to the National Risk Assessment confirm that corruption remains among the highest\nML threats. Therefore, it is particularly relevant in the context of Mexico to further refine the\nunderstanding of risks of laundering of domestic corruption to highlight the key sectors at risk and\nthe macroeconomic impact of those crimes. Such understanding of risks of corruption and\nlaundering of its proceeds could help better inform policy determination under the AML and AC\nframeworks and ensure their better integration and complementarity.\n\n3.      The recognition of corruption as a substantial problem in Mexico led to the 2015\nreforms. The National Anti-Corruption System (Sistema Nacional Anticorrupción or SNA) was\nintroduced following amendments to the constitution, along with a package of new laws and legal\namendments that provide for the regulatory and institutional framework in the fight against\ncorruption. 3 The SNA is coordinated by the SNA Coordination Committee comprising seven\n\n1 Prepared by Adrian Wardzynski, with research support from Alessandro D’Amelio (both LEG).\n\n2 Countries that struggle to tackle take decisive actions against corruption usually have lower levels of compliance\n\nwith AML standards. See C. Verdugo, Compliance with the AML/CFT International Standard: Lessons from a Cross-\nCountry Analysis, IMF Working Paper, WP/11/177.\n3 In addition to changes to Article 113 of the Constitution, the AC legal package included the General Law on the\n\nNational Anti-Corruption System (Ley General del Sistema Nacional Anticorrupción), the General Law on\nAdministrative Responsibilities (Ley General de Responsabilidades Administrativas), the Law of Control and\nAccountability of the Federation (Ley de Fiscalización y Rendición de Cuentas de la Federación), amendments to the\nOrganic Law of the Federal Court of Administrative Justice (Ley Orgánica del Tribunal Federal de Justicia\nAdministrativa), amendments to the Organic Law of the Attorney’s General Office (Ley Orgánica de la Fiscalía General\nde la República), amendments to the Federal Penal Code (Código Penal Federal), amendments to the Organic Law of\nFederal Public Administration (Ley Orgánica de la Administración Pública Federal).\n\n\n84   INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND\n\nMEXICO\n\n\n\nnational institutions. The Financial Intelligence Unit (FIU) and the AML supervisors, i.e., the National\nBanking and Securities Commission (Comisión Nacional Bancaria y de Valores or CNBV) and the tax\nauthority (Servicio de Administración Tributaria or SAT), are indirectly represented at the Committee\nthrough the Ministry of Public Administration (MPA).\n\n4.      Policies of the SNA could reflect better the high risk of laundering of proceeds of\ncorruption. The SNA Coordination Committee approved the National Anti-Corruption Policy\n(Prioridades e implementación de la Política Nacional or PNA) in January 2020 and the National\nImplementation Plan in January 2022. The overall effectiveness of the PNA will also depend on the\nstrength of Mexico’s AML framework, especially given that corruption is among the highest ML\nthreats in Mexico. As part of operationalization of these policies, the priorities for the PNA and its\nimplementation plan benchmarks should reflect AML measures and close coordination with AML\nauthorities.\n\n5.      Coordinating policy priorities for AML and AC could achieve better outcomes. Such\ncoordination could ensure common understanding of risks of corruption and ML and the\nvulnerabilities of different sectors for misuse, better allocation of resources, and prioritization of\nboth frameworks to achieve common outcomes. At the operational level, the MPA, FIU, and AML\nsupervisors should exchange information and cooperate by drawing on the synergies between their\nmandates and relevant information which would enable taking joint action against corruption and\nits proceeds. Given the importance of financial intelligence produced by the FIU to detect and\ndisrupt proceeds of corruption, as well as the role of banks and other financial and non-financial\ngatekeepers in preventing access of “dirty money” into the formal and informal economy, it is\nimportant that the AML and AC policies are coordinated to ensure that the mutually reinforcing\neffects of the AML and AC frameworks are fully utilized.\n\nB. Laundering of Proceeds of Corruption and AML Preventative Measures\n\n6.      PEPs are subject to enhanced due diligence under the AML framework in Mexico. The\nAML financial sector supervisor in Mexico, the CNBV, has dedicated criteria that it uses to verify\nwhether banks and other financial institutions under its supervisory purview are compliant with the\napplicable preventive measures for PEPs. The CNBV has also published guidance to financial entities\non the risks of corruption and related money laundering, which includes details on the necessary\ncontrols related to PEPs. There is also a list of PEPs published by the Ministry of Finance and Public\nCredit to further aid compliance by regulated entities.\n\n7.       The implementation of AML controls applicable to PEPs could be further strengthened\nand the major gaps in the legal framework of the non-financial sector addressed. The CNBV\nfound numerous non-compliance issues in enhanced due diligence processes applied by financial\ninstitutions in relation to PEPs. These include inadequate risk assessment and monitoring of\ntransactions to ensure alignment with the roles, level, and responsibilities of PEPs, failures to\ndetermine PEPs that may be controlling legal entities, reliance on outdated lists of PEPs, as well as\nother documentation and quality of information issues. While these are primarily about aspects of\n\n\n\n                                                                   INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND       85\n\nMEXICO\n\n\n\neffective implementation, the regulatory framework in the non-financial sector is not yet fully in\nplace. Currently, the Designated Non-Financial Businesses and Professions (DNFBPs)—such as\nlawyers, notaries, accountants, real estate agents, or Trust and Company Service Providers—are not\nlegally obliged under Mexico’s AML legal framework to carry out due diligence checks in relation to\nPEPs. 4 This is particularly important given the key role that such gatekeepers play in laundering\nproceeds of corruption through activities such as running and managing of business activities of\nlegal entities or purchasing of expensive real estate, among the most common techniques to\nlaunder corruption proceeds in Mexico.\n\n8.      STRs filed by the AML regulated entities are an important aspect of detecting financial\ncrimes, including corruption. The FIU indicated that it receives more than 340,000 STRs per year.\nThese cover suspicions of money laundering and its underlying financial crimes such as corruption\nor tax crimes.\n\n9.       Mexico's legal framework for STRs needs significant improvements, especially in\nrelation to DNFBPs. Most of the technical deficiencies in relation to the STRs identified in the\nFinancial Action Task Force (FATF) Mutual Evaluation Report published in 2018 remain unresolved. 5\nIn the case of the STRs obligations applicable to financial institutions, there are issues with the\napplicable timeframes and the level of suspicion required to trigger filing. In the case of the DNFBPs,\nthe existing obligation to file Notices should be amended to ensure comprehensive coverage and\nalignment with the FATF Recommendations. This translates directly into the low levels of reports\nfiled by the DNFBPs and the inability to effectively sanction such practices. More broadly, the overall\nAML regulatory and supervisory framework over the DNFBPs requires substantial strengthening. 6\n\n10.      Mexico should address deficiencies in the implementation of the STR obligations and\nboost the capacity of the FIU to prioritize the STRs related to PEPs. In addition to addressing\nthe legal deficiencies, the implementation of STR obligations should be further strengthened. Based\non the supervisory activities of the CNBV, there are recurrent issues in the accuracy of the customer\ndata that can compromise the integrity and reliability of the client records and related filing\nobligations. Further deficiencies were found by the CNBV in the application of the minimum criteria\nfor the filing of reports under the “24-hours process” and the quality of information included in the\nSTRs (i.e., the description of the underlying operations and the reasons for the suspicions). The FIU\nshould further promote compliance with best practices on the filing of STRs, including those related\nto PEPs and suspicious activities related to laundering generated by corruption, and prioritize\nensuing compliance and raising awareness of the DNBFPs. Given the large number of reports\nreceived by the FIU, the authorities should also ensure that it has adequate resources, both human\nand technological, to carry out sufficient screening of the received reports while prioritizing the STRs\nrelated to PEPs.\n\n4 See the Financial Action Task Force Follow-up Report on Mexico published in May 2023 and available at\n\nhttps://www.fatf-gafi.org/content/dam/fatf-gafi/fur/Follow-Up-Report-Mexico-2023.pdf.coredownload.pdf.\n5 For the 2018 Mutual Evaluation Report, see https://www.fatf-gafi.org/en/publications/Mutualevaluations/Mer-\n\nmexico-2018.html.\n6 See the 2023 Follow-Up Report available at https://www.fatf-gafi.org/content/dam/fatf-gafi/fur/Follow-Up-Report-\n\nMexico-2023.pdf.coredownload.pdf.\n\n\n86   INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND\n\nMEXICO\n\n\n\n11.      Misuse of legal entities is among the techniques most used in Mexico to launder\nproceeds of crimes, including corruption. Shell companies and front companies are commonly\nutilized to conceal the beneficial ownership of assets and funds as well as the true purpose of\ntransactions. These are also used by PEPs in Mexico to cover payment of bribes and embezzle public\nfunds.\n\n12.      Implementation of the AML framework in Mexico does not adequately ensure\navailability of beneficial ownership information. The AML framework requires that regulated\nentities identify beneficial ownership information as part of their customer due diligence processes.\nThere can be however doubts about the veracity of this source of beneficial ownership information\nin light of the effectiveness issues. 7 As identified by the CNBV, there are significant issues in the\nprocedures followed by financial institutions to identify beneficial owners including for PEPs.\nDeficiencies have been identified in the collection and review of the necessary data and\ndocumentation that is essential for this largely factual test. The difficulties in reliance on the\nbeneficial ownership information obtained through regulated entities were also acknowledged by\nthe FIU that requires it for the purposes of financial analysis and production of financial intelligence\nreports for law enforcement agencies. The prosecutors generally rely on their own analysis—\nincluding review of the legal structure, board minutes, and field-based investigations—for the\ndetermination of beneficial ownership information. Given the key value of beneficial ownership\ninformation in tackling financial crimes, the authorities should step up supervisory oversight and\napply proportional and dissuasive sanctions in cases of non-compliance. There are also significant\nissues in the legal framework applicable to the DNFBPs, including in relation to the understanding of\nthe legal and control structure of the customers, which should be addressed through legislative\nchanges.\n\n13.     The new beneficial ownership information requirements arising under the tax laws are\ncurrently underutilized for AML purposes. The amendments to the Federal Tax Code in\nNovember 2021, which took effect in January 2022, introduced new obligations pursuant to Article\n32-B Ter on legal entities and their service providers to obtain and report beneficial ownership\ninformation to the Mexican tax authorities (SAT). These new rules were introduced to enable the SAT\nto obtain such information upon request and then report to foreign tax authorities in compliance\nwith requirements arising under the international tax transparency standards overseen by the Global\nForum on Transparency and Exchange of Information. 8 While these may also be of value for the\nbroader entity transparency objectives and tackling of financial crimes, they have not been used to\ndate in this context. Moreover, given the specific rationale for the introduction of these new\nobligations, there is no specific database that can be consulted by other competent authorities and\nregulated entities at the SAT for the beneficial ownership information that it has the ability to\nrequest. The SAT also does not verify the information. Mexico should introduce a beneficial\n\n7 For an analysis regarding the overall low effectiveness of the AML preventive measures, including those relating to\n\nbeneficial ownership requirements, see the 2018 Mutual Evaluation Report of Mexico adopted by the FATF and\navailable at https://www.fatf-gafi.org/en/countries/detail/Mexico.html.\n8 See https://www.oecd.org/publications/global-forum-on-transparency-and-exchange-of-information-for-tax-\n\npurposes-mexico-2023-second-round-6fd9ab78-en.htm.\n\n\n\n                                                                            INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND            87\n\nMEXICO\n\n\n\nownership registry and establish relevant processes to ensure that the information collected is\naccurate, adequate, and up to date. Beneficial ownership information should also be required to be\ncollected and verified as part of the public procurement process.\n\nC. Prosecution of Money Laundering and Corruption\n\n14.      The jurisdiction and cooperation between the specialized prosecutor offices\nresponsible for money laundering and corruption in Mexico should be enhanced. Money\nlaundering cases are prosecuted by the Specialized Prosecutor Office for Organized Crime (Fiscalía\nEspecializada en Materia de Delincuencia Organizada or FEMDO). This office can also carry out\nparallel investigations of money laundering and other financial crimes, such as corruption, if they are\nconsidered of high impact. In 2019 the Specialized Prosecutor Office for Corruption (Fiscalía\nEspecializada en Combate a la Corrupción or FMCCO) was established. While this office was\nexpected to take the lead on corruption-related cases, it has mainly dealt with matters relating to\nabuse of office, which is one of the forms of corruption. Corruption and bribery cases are not among\nits top five categories of cases and it has not yet prosecuted any high-level corruption cases. The\nauthorities should clarify the jurisdiction between the FEMDO and FEMCCO and also provide for\ntheir close cooperation to avoid fragmentation that would adversely affect the effectiveness to\nprosecute corruption cases and related money laundering.\n\n15.     The authorities should ensure that there are strict AC policies applicable to the law\nenforcement authorities, at both federal and state levels, as well as the judiciary. The level of\ncorruption in Mexico can affect the functioning of the law enforcement system and undermines the\ncapacity to investigate and prosecute corruption and money laundering. The number prosecutions\nand convictions relating to corruption and money laundering is not commensurate with Mexico’s\nrisk profile. The authorities should take steps to increase resources, capacity, and expertise as well as\nensure adequate budget of the law enforcement authorities. Confiscation and asset recovery should\nbe seen as major policy objectives within the national AML and AC policies and implementation\nplans in order to ensure that crime does not pay.\n\n\n\n\n88   INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND", "excerpt_sha256": "cbaeabc12eb75d780c6f0f351a804d89cb38d728fd290435b2a3739b815d8dca", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0016-E022", "source_id": "SRC-684988a395d1a128", "url": "https://www.banxico.org.mx/publications-and-press/imf-article-iv-consultation-for-mexico/%7B2B9230B7-CC63-5B20-E173-F5E94D116BC9%7D.pdf", "title": "IMF Country Report No. 23/356 — Mexico", "publisher": "Banco de México / International Monetary Fund", "raw_sha256": "f93592f437a264c95c4c753720221642f0dd636d55e012cef97b95d1b59a294a", "fact_summary": "The IMF found limited foreign-bribery risk but reported no completed foreign-bribery prosecution, inadequate whistleblower protection, unclear corporate liability, and unresolved deficiencies in PEP monitoring, beneficial-ownership identification, suspicious-transaction reporting, and DNFBP supervision. It acknowledged legal and institutional reforms and broader PEP controls while calling for effective enforcement.", "question_ids": ["5B.5", "5B.7", "7B.10", "8B.6"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "The supplementary information updates the assessment as of October 2023.", "ruler_attribution": "The findings concern Mexican authorities and institutions under AMLO; they do not establish personal conduct by López Obrador.", "limitations": ["The source explicitly notes that some information supplied by Mexico was not independently verified by the OECD monitoring body."], "start_unit": 103, "end_unit": 105, "locator": "page 103 through page 105", "exact_excerpt": "MEXICO\n\n\n\nTRANSNATIONAL ASPECTS OF CORRUPTION1\nA. Introduction 2\n1.       Foreign bribery risks are relatively limited but given the export-driven nature of the\neconomy, further mitigating efforts are called for. Among the 500 largest multinational\nenterprises (MNEs) in the world, only one multinational enterprise (MNE) is headquartered in\nMexico. It is also not among the major sources of foreign direct investment to the rest of the world. 3\nMexico has engaged in anti-bribery institutional and legal reforms and enhanced awareness of\npublic officials to report foreign bribery. However, there has been no prosecution of foreign bribery\ncases. It is advisable for the authorities to take further measures, including to ensure adequate\nprotection of reporting persons and promote effective enforcement.\n\n2.        While risks of laundering of proceeds of foreign corruption in Mexico are overall\nlimited, they should be further mitigated. There is no indication of major threats of laundering of\nproceeds of foreign corruption in Mexico and the AML framework already includes several\nmitigating measures to detect and disrupt such proceeds including preventive measures in the\nfinancial sector focused on politically exposed persons (PEPs). This could be further enhanced by\nfacilitating reporting of suspicious activities from the key gatekeepers, including in the non-financial\nsector (e.g., real estate agents, lawyers, accountants), which should also cover suspicions in relation\nto PEPs. The latter would require introducing the relevant regulatory into the preventive and\nsupervisory framework for the non-financial sector, which should be seen as a priority. The\nauthorities should further continue improving the availability, accuracy, and access to beneficial\nownership information of companies to prevent their misuse for the laundering of foreign proceeds\nof corruption. 4\n\nB. Supply Side of Corruption 5\n3.    Mexico has taken steps to enhance its anti-bribery legal and institutional framework,\nthough more efforts are needed to address foreign bribery. It has engaged in important\n\n1 Prepared by Yao Deng and Adrian Wardzynski (both LEG).\n\n2 Mexico volunteered to have its legal and institutional frameworks assessed in the context of bilateral surveillance\n\nfor purposes of determining whether it: (a) criminalizes and prosecutes the bribery of foreign public officials; and (b)\nhas an effective AML/CFT system that is designed to prevent foreign officials from concealing the proceeds of\ncorruption.\n3 See OECD- UNSD Multinational Enterprise Information Platform, and International Financial Statistics International\n\nInvestment Position, Assets, Direct investment.\n4 For an analysis regarding the overall low effectiveness of the preventive measures set out in the AML framework see\n\nthe 2018 Mutual Evaluation Report of Mexico adopted by the FATF and available at https://www.fatf-\ngafi.org/en/countries/detail/Mexico.html.\n5 Information relating to supply-side corruption in this annex draws on the WGB’s Phase 4 Report of Mexico (October\n\n2018), the follow-up report (August 2022, updated) and the additional written report (June 2023). The IMF staff and\nMexico have provided additional views and information whose accuracy have not been verified by the WGB or the\nOECD Secretariat, and which do not prejudice the WGB’s monitoring of the implementation of the OECD Anti-Bribery\nConvention.\n\n\n2    INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND\n\nMEXICO\n\n\n\ninstitutional and legal reforms in the fight against foreign bribery, including amending the foreign\nbribery offense to be applicable to cases involving third party beneficiaries, making corporate\nliability for foreign bribery independent of the liability of the individual perpetrators, increasing the\nmaximum sanctions for accounting offenses, and making bribes non-tax-deductible. In addition, it\nhas initiated judicial statistical collection aiming to provide comprehensive information about\nforeign bribery enforcement and introduced successor liability into the criminal corporate liability\nframework. Mexico further took efforts to raise awareness of public officials of their obligation to\nreport instances of foreign bribery through the provision of training. While acknowledging this\nprogress, the 2022 follow-up report of the OECD Working Group on Bribery in International Business\nTransactions (WGB) expressed concerns over the adequacy of Mexico’s efforts to address foreign\nbribery, especially given the export driven nature of the Mexican economy and the inclusion of high-\nrisk sectors for corruption in exports, such as extractives, manufacturing, and agricultural products.\n\n4.      Looking forward, Mexico is encouraged to take further measures, inter alia, to ensure\nadequate whistleblower protection, clarify liability of legal persons, and enhance effective\nenforcement against foreign bribery. A specific law providing for whistleblower protection of\npublic and private sector employees that report suspicions of foreign bribery has not been enacted,\nthough a draft bill was reported as being under discussion. 6 In addition, obstacles to reporting by\ngovernment agencies remain; for example, the tax authorities have difficulties in reporting or\nsharing suspicions of foreign bribery. While the liability of legal persons for foreign bribery is not\nclear under the current legal framework and the coverage of foreign bribery cases by the General\nLaw on Administrative Responsibility needs to be further confirmed, the State-Owned Enterprises\ncannot be held liable for foreign bribery. 7 Most notably, however, Mexico is yet to successfully\nconclude its first foreign bribery case. The lack of proactive investigative measures in foreign bribery\ninvestigations poses challenges. According to the data available, there were only a few foreign\nbribery investigations in Mexico which did not progress to the prosecution stage. It will be\nimportant to take stock of the impact of the nomination of a Specialized Prosecutor for Combatting\nCorruption (FECC) since 2019 on the investigation and prosecution of foreign bribery. The\nauthorities should take additional steps to prioritize detection, enforcement, and effective\nsanctioning of foreign bribery cases in line with the OECD WGB’s Phase 4 recommendations.\n\nC. Facilitation of Proceeds of Foreign Corruption (Concealment-Side)\n\n5.      Mexico’s AML regime helps prevent laundering of foreign proceeds of corruption. The\n2020 National Risk Assessment (NRA) carried out by Mexico recognized corruption, including\ntransnational corruption, as a structural risk. Specific guidance was subsequently released on\ncorruption risks along with several related typologies. The definition of Politically Exposed Persons\n(PEPs), which includes domestic and foreign PEPs, was further broadened following the 2018 Mutual\n\n6 The discussion on the draft was reported by Mexico in its 2023 Additional Written Report.\n\n7 In the Additional Written Report submitted to the OECD Working Group on Bribery in June 2023, Mexico stated\n\nthat the criminal and administrative liabilities of legal persons belong to two different areas. Therefore, article 25 of\nthe General Law of Administrative Responsibilities (LGRA) cannot be applied in the context of a criminal proceeding\nthat involves a legal entity pursuant to article 421 of the National Code of Criminal Procedures.\n\n\n\n                                                                               INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND              3\n\nMEXICO\n\n\n\nEvaluation Report (MER) of Mexico to ensure its alignment with the FATF Recommendations and the\nMinistry of Finance and Public Credit issues a list of public officials that are considered PEPs. 8 The\nPEP provisions provide for enhanced due diligence, including close monitoring and possibility of\nverification of the source of funds and wealth. The CNBV has specific inspection criteria relating to\ncorruption and beneficial ownership customer due diligence obligations imposed on certain\nfinancial institutions. The CNBV and Financial Intelligence Unit (FIU) also published guidelines on\nprevention and detection of corruption, including the risks and specific requirements relating to\nPEPs. Recently, there were further improvements in the Federal Tax Code relating to the availability\nof beneficial ownership information in Mexico that carry the potential to enhance the capacity of the\nauthorities to prevent misuse of legal vehicles in concealing the ownership of illicit assets and\nproceeds, including those stemming from corruption. Mexico has also updated its NRA to expand its\nunderstanding of risks regarding the potential for misuse of the different types of legal persons\nbased on complaints, criminal investigations, prosecutions, and convictions, as well as transnational\nrequests.\n\n6.        The authorities should continue their efforts to prevent laundering of foreign\nproceeds of corruption by bolstering the applicability of the relevant AML measures. As part\nof its risk-based supervision carried out by the CNBV identified irregularities in the application of the\nenhanced due diligence applicable to PEPs, several issues with the effective implementation of the\nenhanced due diligence with respect to PEPs were identified. These include issues with effective\nmonitoring of PEPs in line with their risk profile as well as issues with the identification and\nobtaining of information on PEPs that are beneficial owners. As far as beneficial ownership is\nconcerned, significant deficiencies have also been found by the CNBV in the development of\nprocedures and identification of beneficial owners by financial institutions, including collection of\nthe necessary data and documentation. The CNBV further found that the STRs submitted by\nfinancial institutions do not always include all the relevant fields and improperly implement best\npractices issued by the FIU. The obligations regarding filing of Notices by the DNFBPs need to be\namended to ensure compliance with the FATF Recommendations, particularly FATF\nRecommendation 23, and thus the relevant legal reform pending in the Mexican Congress should be\napproved and enacted without any further delays. 9 In the same vein, the authorities should, as a\nmatter of priority, introduce the regulatory and supervisory framework over the DNFBPs in line with\nthe risk-based approach mandated by the international standards. As part of its risk-based\nsupervisory priorities, the CNBV should put greater emphasis on the risk of misuse of legal entities\nto conceal payment of bribes and cross-border transactions relating to possible corruption\nproceeds. The authorities should pursue parallel money laundering, corruption, and related\norganized crimes investigations and prosecutions including when corruption is committed abroad,\nand cases of non-compliance should be subject to effective sanctions mechanisms.                                        INFORME DE ACTIVIDADES 2023\n\n\n\n\n                             Hechos violatorios                                    Frecuencia\n\nAcciones y omisiones que transgreden los derechos de los migrantes y de\n                                                                                        2\nsus familiares\n\nEfectuar una detención sin contar con la orden correspondiente, fuera de\n                                                                                        2\nlos casos de flagrancia\n\nImputar indebidamente hechos                                                            2\n\nIncumplir con alguna de las formalidades para la emisión de la orden de\n                                                                                        2\ncateo o durante la ejecución de este, así como para las visitas domiciliarias\n\nAcciones y omisiones contrarias a la legalidad                                           1\n\nActos y omisiones contrarios a la vida familiar                                          1\n\nActos y omisiones que transgreden el derecho a la reparación de los daños,\n                                                                                         1\nperjuicios o daño moral, por parte de los órganos del poder público\n\nArraigar a una persona en lugar distinto al previsto por la ley o en\n
